wix-3060a10e-6c2a-48f0-87c4-70707de56ca9-7159514c-6ce8-4533-bb8d-c1df5e1edc61	Lietzensee - Berlin Art Print — 30x40 cm	Physical product. Selected option: Size: 30x40 cm. Lietzensee in Charlottenburg looks like two lakes from above. In 1904, a road embankment cut across its narrow waist. The water still connects beneath the Lietzenseebrücke. This print turns that story into a simple paper-collage composition: cobalt-blue water, a burnt-orange road and a fine turquoise connection on warm cream. The pale-blue shadow is an artistic echo, not a traced historical shoreline. It is a stylised artwork, not a measured map. Printed to order on premium matte 200 gsm paper. Choose 30 x 40 cm or 50 x 70 cm. The 30 x 40 cm print runs to the edges. The 50 x 70 cm print places the complete 45 x 60 cm artwork on a light mount, with 25 mm at each side and 50 mm at the top and bottom. The artwork is not cropped or stretched. Unframed paper print. wix-8a4fc4d9-76f1-4cd1-a4cd-ea6d20190157-5a1ebb80-6391-402c-b6d5-b1bf8e537c0d	Hackescher Markt - Berlin Art Print — 30x40 cm	Physical product. Selected option: Size: 30x40 cm. The S-Bahn does not run straight through Hackescher Markt. It swings in a long curve on its brick arches, and this print shows that curve from above: one red and cream train on the viaduct, terracotta and ochre arches below, deep petrol shadows on warm cream paper. The pale blue line under the arches is the old fortress moat. The railway here was built on its filled bed, and the street beside the station is still called Am Zwirngraben. The blue is a memory, not water you can see today. This is a stylised illustration, not a measured map: the arches, the tracks and the blocks around them are simplified. Printed to order on premium matte 200 gsm paper. Choose 30 x 40 cm or 50 x 70 cm. The 30 x 40 cm print runs to the edges. On the 50 x 70 cm print the same artwork sits on a light paper mount, 25 mm at the sides and 50 mm at the top and bottom, so nothing is cropped or stretched. Unframed paper print. wix-b35df559-a0f9-407c-859a-ca2a4176b6df-a2d0f894-5d9c-4f17-a86a-bec4bff77c72	Leipziger Platz - Berlin Art Print — 30x40 cm	Physical product. Selected option: Size: 30x40 cm. Leipziger Platz is a real octagon, and this print shows it from above in dark petrol, ochre, rust red and sand. I kept the shape of the square and let the colours carry its history: the dark petrol ring stands for the blocks around it today, the ochre lines for the older block line, the broken rust-red band for the ruins of 1945 and the three cranes for the building site after 1990. The ring opens only where the square opens today, to the east towards Leipziger Straße and to the west towards Potsdamer Platz. The layers are symbolic: this is a stylised illustration, not a measured map, and it does not show where the Wall ran. Printed to order on premium matte 200 gsm paper. Choose 30 x 40 cm or 50 x 70 cm. The complete artwork is preserved without cropping or stretching; the 50 x 70 cm version shows a little more of the surrounding blocks at the top and bottom. wix-f43b6ad7-a7ee-4df6-bedb-591d934c6f96-40e2ca2a-5858-4158-9a3b-a0a841aadf7a	Markthallen - Berlin Art Print — 30x40 cm	Physical product. Selected option: Size: 30x40 cm. Fourteen small market halls in dark green and terracotta on warm ochre paper. Between 1886 and 1892 Berlin opened fourteen numbered municipal market halls, I to XIV, and this poster gives each of them one building, in order from left to right and top to bottom. The filled halls are the four that still sell food today (VI Ackerhalle, IX Markthalle Neun, X Arminiushalle and XI Marheineke, checked in November 2026), and the outlines are the ten that no longer do. The count and the four filled halls come from my own research on Berlin's market halls. The building icons are stylised, not exact facades. Printed to order on premium matte 200 gsm paper. Choose 30 x 40 cm or 50 x 70 cm. The complete artwork is preserved without cropping or stretching, and the ochre paper colour runs to the edges. Unframed paper print. Type your address in Berlin and see your street from above in 1928, the way a survey plane photographed it, with the same spot today right below it, at the same scale. Move the cross onto your building, add a line of your own, and I print it for you. Most Berlin Altbau blocks were already standing in 1928, so you can often find your own courtyard. If your house is newer, you will see what stood there before it. That is part of the story too. A good gift for someone who just moved in, for a couple's first flat, or for anyone leaving Berlin who wants to take their Kiez with them. What you get A 30 x 40 or 45 x 60 cm art print on the paper you pick, unframed. wix-08a254a1-9cdb-4cb0-9c0c-098409bcf230-1ee72652-5c3e-4896-bab1-4d2f20e3b0d4	Osman Kalın’s Treehouse Berlin Art Print — Berlin Reels Collection — 30x40 cm, Premium Matte	Physical product. Selected option: Size: 30x40 cm, Paper: Premium Matte. At Bethaniendamm in Kreuzberg the Wall was built straight across a bend in the border, and a triangle of East Berlin land of about 350 square metres ended up on the western side. Nobody looked after it. In 1983 Osman Kalın, a retired guest worker with six children, cleared the rubbish and planted vegetables. East German border guards checked that he was not digging an escape tunnel, then let him stay. His hut had to stay lower than the Wall. After the Wall fell, the district that inherited the land called his garden illegal and told him to leave. His neighbours, the pastor of St. Thomas Church and the Kreuzberg district stood up for him. The house burned down in 1991 and again in 2003 and was rebuilt both times. He died in April 2018. This print follows a photograph from 2014, with the line from my reel: EAST BERLIN LAND. WEST OF THE WALL. Go and look, it is free and it is real. Bethaniendamm, corner of Mariannenplatz. When I checked on 6 September 2026 it was a home, not a museum yet, so look from the street and do not climb the fence. WEST OF THE WALL. Go and look, it is free and it is real. Bethaniendamm, corner of Mariannenplatz. When I checked on 6 September 2026 it was a home, not a museum yet, so look from the street and do not climb the fence. At Bethaniendamm in Kreuzberg the Wall was built straight across a bend in the border, and a triangle of East Berlin land of about 350 square metres ended up on the western side. wix-6722fada-c4d9-4ac0-ab8a-6e2c6e911823-637aa5a7-b7f8-4f10-ba49-700242148bfa	Ghost Stations Berlin Art Print — Berlin Reels Collection — 30x40 cm, Premium Matte	Physical product. Selected option: Size: 30x40 cm, Paper: Premium Matte. From 13 August 1961, three lines that connected parts of West Berlin, the U6, the U8 and the north-south S-Bahn, kept running through tunnels under East Berlin. The stations up there were sealed on the surface and guarded below, and the trains went through slowly, without stopping. Passengers called them Geisterbahnhöfe, ghost stations. This poster is the line from my reel as one picture: THE TRAINS DID NOT STOP. It is a reconstruction, not a photograph of a sealed platform. Jannowitzbrücke was the first ghost station to reopen, on 11 November 1989, two days after the Wall opened. The architecture in the picture comes from a photograph taken there a week later. The Berlin Wall Foundation runs a free exhibition on the border and ghost stations on the mezzanine of Nordbahnhof S-Bahn station, open during the station's opening hours. I would give it twenty minutes on any Berlin trip. Then ride the U8 through Mitte and notice that every station stops for you. Selected option: Size: 45x60 cm, Paper: Premium Matte. From 13 August 1961, three lines that connected parts of West Berlin, the U6, the U8 and the north-south S-Bahn, kept running through tunnels under East Berlin. The stations up there were sealed on the surface and guarded below, and the trains went through slowly, without stopping. Passengers called them Geisterbahnhöfe, ghost stations. This poster is the line from my reel as one picture: THE TRAINS DID NOT STOP. It is a reconstruction, not a photograph of a sealed platform. Jannowitzbrücke was the first ghost station to reopen, on 11 November 1989, two days after the Wall opened. The architecture in the picture comes from a photograph taken there a week later. The Berlin Wall Foundation runs a free exhibition on the border and ghost stations on the mezzanine of Nordbahnhof S-Bahn station, open during the station's opening hours. I would give it twenty minutes on any Berlin trip. Then ride the U8 through Mitte and notice that every station stops for you. wix-7c2f473b-ecbd-4a19-9d1f-18a12fa56652-2dc5bf35-c748-4a79-9b03-a1c51e00c327	Berlin Tram Map and Wall Art Print — Berlin Reels Collection — 30x40 cm, Premium Matte	Physical product. Selected option: Size: 30x40 cm, Paper: Premium Matte. Open a Berlin tram map today and you can still see the Wall. This print puts that sentence on paper: the tram routes of September 2026 in yellow, the course of the Wall in 1989 in red. The yellow is today's network, not the one from 1989. Most of it sits east of the red line, and that is how the old border still shows. West Berlin chose the U-Bahn, buses and the car over the tram, and its last tram ran on 2 October 1967. The East kept its trams and pushed them out to the new estates. In my reel the red line followed the edge of the western districts. For this print I used the mapped course of the Wall in 1989 from Geoportal Berlin instead; it is an illustrated map, not a survey. The U-Bahn is a separate story: its map shows the Wall the other way round. Go to U Turmstraße in Moabit. A tram came back there on 9 September 2023, almost 56 years after the last one ran in West Berlin, and as I write the line still ends at that stop. Stand there and look at where the rails stop. wix-1e5af6a4-4628-4f6a-adfb-744e2fc2049e-0d375498-aee6-47e0-bb22-a38ef3361fe6	Berlin-Uhr Art Print — Berlin Reels Collection — 30x40 cm, Premium Matte	Physical product. Selected option: Size: 30x40 cm, Paper: Premium Matte. In 1975 Berlin put a clock on the Kurfürstendamm that takes a minute to learn to read. The Berlin-Uhr went up on 17 June 1975 on the middle strip at Uhlandstraße. Dieter Binninger designed it, and the Guinness Book of Records listed it as the first clock in the world to show the time with coloured lights. Berliners call it the Mengenlehreuhr, the set theory clock. It counts in fives. All those bulbs made it costly to keep alive. Binninger died in 1991, the clock went out of service in 1995, and in 1996 it went back up in front of the Europa-Center on Budapester Straße, where it stands today. This print freezes it at 13:21, so the lights are the whole picture. Next time you are at Breitscheidplatz, give it one minute. Count the red lamps in the top row first, five hours each: two make 10. The next row is one hour a lamp: three more make 13. Then the long row, five minutes a lamp: four make 20, and one single minute makes 21. This print freezes it at 13:21, so the lights are the whole picture. Next time you are at Breitscheidplatz, give it one minute. Count the red lamps in the top row first, five hours each: two make 10. The next row is one hour a lamp: three more make 13. Then the long row, five minutes a lamp: four make 20, and one single minute makes 21.
